 SECTION 1.0
 INTRODUCTION

1.1 Background

 Databases of high-level waste (HLW) and low activity waste (LAW) glass properties and
compositions have been collected at the Vitreous State Laboratory (VSL) to support the
vitrification processes at the Hanford Tank Waste Treatment and Immobilization Plant (WTP).
The databases have been used to develop HLW and LAW glass property-composition models
that are intended to control the vitrification process at WTP to ensure the HLW and LAW glasses
will meet their respective product quality and processability requirements. The quality and the
validity ranges of the property-composition models will greatly influence waste treatment
operations, including the waste loading that is achievable for each waste composition. The
baseline models for WTP operation were developed under the Bechtel National, Inc. (BNI) WTP
contract [1-4]. These models were based on HLW and LAW glass property data collected over a
range of compositions that was guided primarily by the WTP contract and the specific set of
tanks specified therein.

 Since the development of the baseline WTP models, the Office of River Protection (ORP)
has directed a program of work at VSL to examine various approaches for optimization of the
performance of the WTP, including increasing the waste loadings in both HLW and LAW
glasses [5–20]. This has resulted in a significant expansion of the compositional regions of the
WTP glasses under study. For HLW glasses, substantial increases in the compositional ranges
have been achieved for many different components ― examples include but are not limited to
aluminum, bismuth, boron, calcium, chromium, iron, manganese, phosphorus, potassium,
sodium, sulfur, and zirconium. As more property-composition data and better information on
HLW compositions became available, part of the program at VSL involved the update,
expansion and enhancement of the HLW glass property-composition models to cover the
enlarged composition spaces. As was the case with the WTP baseline models development,
expansion and enhancement of the various HLW glass property-composition models is a
multi-year effort that employs a phased approach. A compilation of the ORP glass data has been
reported previously together with updated models, the development of which was based on the
WTP baseline models [21-24]. This report presents and discusses the results of glass testing from
the latest enhancement work and the model updates for WTP HLW glasses.

 The HLW glasses to be processed at WTP must meet a variety of melter processing and
product quality requirements before they can be accepted for disposal. Baseline models were
developed to relate various properties to HLW glass compositions and the properties of interest
included Product Consistency Test (PCT) responses for B-, Li-, and Na-releases; spinel
crystallization (one-percent crystal fraction temperature, T1%); melt viscosity; melt electrical
conductivity (EC); and Toxicity Characteristic Leach Procedure (TCLP) Cd responses [1, 2]. No
WTP models were developed to control nepheline formation upon slow cooling (i.e., Canister

 12

Centerline Cooling (CCC)), which is projected to be an important constraint that will limit the
waste loading for many waste types. Instead of developing a new nepheline model, a constraint
based on normalized silica concentration (the nepheline discriminator [25]) was used to address
nepheline formation during canister cooling. Earlier VSL work developed and incorporated new
property-composition data in several phases, expanding the number of HLW glasses in the
database from 305 to 936 [21-24]. Furthermore, the ORP data included nepheline formation
upon CCC heat treatment. Development of a model to predict nepheline formation, however, is
the subject of a separate ORP task. The WTP baseline models and their updates were evaluated
with statistical methods to determine how well the models fit the data used in their development
[2, 22-24]. In general, comparable goodness-of-fit statistics are obtained for most baseline
models and their subsequent updates, in spite of a greatly expanded composition region covered
by the enhancements. By contrast, updates of the spinel T1% model tend to result in poorer fits
when compared to the baseline. All spinel T1% models, including the WTP baseline and updates
developed using the combined WTP/ORP database, are also subject to relatively large prediction
uncertainties.

 The WTP baseline models are “global” models in the sense that each model uses a single
model form over the entire glass composition region of interest. A potential disadvantage of such
models is that their performance may worsen as the compositional spaces expand, yielding less
accurate property predictions. Therefore, in addition to updating the global models, previous
enhancements included the development of “local” models. Local models cover smaller
compositional regions and may give better fits and more accurate predictions. Two sub-regions
were defined for local models development: low alumina (Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt%) and high alumina
(Al2O3 = 13.1 wt% to 29.6 wt%). This choice of division was made over other choices because:
(i) while the WTP glasses were developed primarily for iron-limited wastes, many of the ORP
glasses were formulated for the aluminum and aluminum/sodium-limited wastes, which
represent a large fraction of the overall waste inventory; (ii) the maximum component limit for
Al2O3 in the WTP baseline validity region is 13.1 wt%; and (iii) the design feature for high-
alumina augmentation during the earlier enhancement phases limits the Al2O3 content to a
minimum of 13.1 wt%. Performances of the low-alumina local models were typically
comparable to those of the respective WTP baseline models, which could be partly attributed to
the significant overlap in the modeling datasets. On the other hand, the paucity of available data
limited the initial development of high-alumina local models. Not only was the high alumina
region covered by fewer glasses but, in addition, most of the glasses were actively designed and
thus provided uneven coverage. With the addition of several statistically designed glass matrices,
however, subsequent enhancements produced valuable data to augment the high-alumina region.
The resulting high-alumina model updates, including the spinel T1% model, all showed greatly
improved performances, with better regression and validation statistics than those found for the
updated baseline models. In most cases, they also outperformed the respective WTP baseline
models.

 The present work continues the enhancement of the HLW property-compositions models,
with the primary objective of improving model performance in terms of regression and
validation statistics. The WTP/ORP database was expanded by inclusion of the HLW glass
property-composition data from other VSL tasks conducted for ORP since the completion of the
last enhancement phase. After a review of the database, statistical design was used to produce a

 13

glass matrix. However, instead of filling in compositional gaps, the matrix was designed to place
HLW formulations in spaces where large errors were projected by the current HLW models.
After ORP review and approval of the test matrix, crucible melts were prepared and glass
properties were measured. The HLW glass property-composition models were then updated
using the expanded database. Comparison of the modeling and validation performances was
made between the updated models and the earlier models.

1.2 Objectives

 Since the WTP compositional space of HLW glasses is extremely large, development of
HLW property-composition models is a multi-year task consisting of multiple phases. The
primary focus of this work was to enhance the WTP HLW models of interest including PCT
releases, spinel crystallization (T1%), viscosity, electrical conductivity, and TCLP-Cd response.
Model development to predict nepheline formation upon CCC is the subject of a separate task. In
particular, the earlier work has produced property-composition models for glass melt viscosity
and glass melt electrical conductivity that showed good performance, while models for PCT
releases and spinel crystallization (T1%) required improvement. Therefore, more efforts were
directed in the present work to collect data and improve model performance for HLW glass PCT
releases and spinel crystallization than for other properties. The present work is a continuation of
earlier development phases and is responsive to the applicable Test Plan [26]. The objectives of
this work were to:

 • Incorporate property-composition data from new glasses into the database.

 • Assess the expanded WTP-ORP database and HLW property-composition models.

 • Identify composition spaces in the database that need augmentation. The basis for
 augmentation was aimed at improving the modeling and validation performances of the
 HLW property-composition models, especially models for predicting the PCT releases
 and spinel crystallization.

 • Develop statistically-designed composition matrices to augment the HLW database by
 strategic placement of new glasses in compositional regions where larger model
 prediction errors were evident.

 • Prepare crucible melts of glass compositions from the statistically-designed composition
 matrices and measure the properties of interest.

 • Incorporate the above property-composition data of the matrix glasses into the database.

 • Assess HLW models with extended datasets.

 • Refine HLW property-composition models.

 14

The Catholic University of America Enhancement of HLW Glass Property-Composition Models
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-19R4480-1, Rev. 0

 The models presented in this report will be further revised as efforts continue to expand
 and improve the models based on additional data collection that is planned in the future phases
 of this work.

 15

 SECTION 2.0
 HLW GLASS DATA AND
 DEVELOPMENT OF TEST MATRIX

 Development of the baseline WTP HLW property-composition models was supported
with data collected from various tests under the WTP project since 1998 that satisfy all
applicable quality assurance (QA) requirements [2]. The initial WTP dataset consist of glasses
that were actively designed to treat specific HLW tank waste streams to meet all product quality
and processing requirements, with little or no intent to evenly cover a composition space. To
supplement these glasses, the WTP project also directed the testing of glass compositions that
were developed with statistical experimental design methods. Implemented over a number of
phases, several HLW glass matrices were designed statistically, fabricated and then characterized
to augment the actively designed glasses. The statistically designed glasses provided better and
more even coverage of the compositional space of interest.

 Incorporation of new HLW glass data from the VSL test program for ORP to address the
expansive composition ranges of Hanford waste also include both actively and statistically
designed glasses. Since the conclusion of the previous phase of this work [24], VSL has
developed and tested new glass formulations for ORP to address various test objectives. The
formulations from these new studies were all actively designed; while not all of the glasses were
determined to be compliant with respect to the WTP processing and product requirements, they
are still valuable for modeling. The new formulations are incorporated into the combined
WTP/ORP database herein, expanding, as well as better covering, the existing HLW
compositional space. In contrast to earlier statistical matrix designs, whereby compositional gaps
in the updated database were identified and filled, the present work focused on reducing the
prediction errors of the property-composition models and employed a different design approach.
This section provides a summary of the glass formulations developed since completion of the
earlier modeling phase [24] and describes the development of the test matrix.

2.1 Incorporation of New Property-Composition Data

 Four HLW glass studies have been completed at VSL since the conclusion of the Phase 3
modeling work [24]. Glasses from these studies were all actively designed to meet the various
test objectives and to support melter tests. The active design was adopted to investigate the
effects of waste loadings on glass properties and to identify compositions acceptable for melter
tests. As a result, not all of the developed HLW glass formulations complied with the processing
and product quality requirements. Note that, however, it is desirable to include both compliant
and non-compliant glasses in the development of property-composition models. All glasses were
incorporated into the WTP/ORP database. This subsection summarizes the sources of the new
data and the objectives of the studies that provided the data. Table 2.1 lists these glass studies
and the number of glasses added into the database.

 16

High Waste Loading Glass Formulation Development for High-P HLW [27]

 A number of HLW batches to be processed at WTP have phosphorus concentrations that
extend up to thirteen weight percent (as P2O5) on a calcined solids basis. This work developed
glass formulations with high waste loadings and good processing characteristics to treat a high
phosphorus Hanford HLW stream. Test results showed that crystallization of secondary phases
and sulfate solubility were the primary constraints that limited the achievable waste loading.
Spinel and calcium/sodium phosphate were the major crystalline phases observed. Another
secondary phase that is detected much less frequently in HLW glasses, lithiophosphate, was also
identified.

 Seventeen HLW glass formulations with P2O5 concentrations ranging between 5.00 wt%
and 7.12 wt% were developed and tested at crucible scale. These glasses contained up to
16.55 wt% Fe2O3 and up to 20.51 wt% Na2O. Melter tests were also performed on the DM100
vitrification system with a target glass that complied with all processing and product quality
requirements. The melter tests demonstrated high glass production rates with the high
phosphorus HLW stream, exceeding the WTP HLW baseline requirement by a factor of two.

High Waste Loading Glass Formulation Development for High-Mn HLW [28]

 Manganese is present in Hanford tank waste in a variety of forms including oxides (MnO
and MnO2), carbonate, spinels and hydroxides with iron/nickel, and oxalates. These differences
in manganese form have the potential to affect waste and melter feed rheology, feed processing
rates, and the extent of crystalline phases in the glass product. The form of manganese may also
affect foaming of the glass pool during vitrification, which has been attributed to manganese
oxidation changes. These issues were addressed by melter testing with a Hanford HLW stream
with high manganese concentration.

 Twenty HLW glass formulations were prepared and characterized to identify a high
waste loading glass composition to support the melter testing. In addition to spinel
crystallization, PCT performance also limited the waste loadings in these glasses. Nevertheless, a
high waste loading glass with over 5 wt% MnO that met all relevant requirements was developed
to support melter tests. Melter tests that employed high-manganese simulants with different
manganese forms were conducted on the DM-100. Glass production rates were found to be at or
below the WTP HLW baseline requirement and showed the following melt rate trend: MnO2 <
MnCO3 < MnO. These results highlight the importance of evaluating the effects of all probable
forms of waste components on the vitrification process.

Glass Formulation Development for Al, Fe, and Na Phosphate HLW [29]

 This work focused on high phosphorus HLW streams and the effects of various forms of
phosphate (aluminum, iron, and sodium) on waste loading, secondary phase formation and

 17

processing characteristics. Testing included preparation and characterization of fifteen crucible
melts to study the effects of waste loading on glass properties and the maximum waste loadings
that can be achieved. Glasses with P2O5 concentrations as high as 6.5 wt% were formulated to
meet all processing and product quality constraints. Many of the formulated glasses, however,
were more corrosive than typical HLW glasses because of the high sodium and phosphorus
contents, both of which originated from the waste. Crucible scale K-3 refractory corrosion
testing provided results to support selection of a target formulation for the subsequent melter
tests. Since the acceptability limit for K-3 refractory corrosion of HLW glasses was not defined
by WTP specifications, the limit recommended for LAW glass formulation work for ORP was
adopted.

 During melter testing and idling of the melter in between tests, the high phosphorus target
glass was found to be highly corrosive to Inconel 690 metal. Corrosion of Inconel components
including the bubbler, thermowell, and airlift lance was discovered after testing high phosphorus
feeds. Moreover, the effects of phosphate and phosphate form (Na3PO4 and FePO4 were tested)
on the glass production rate were shown to be significant. Additional glass formulation
development for similar wastes will be required to identify compositions that have acceptable
corrosion (towards Inconel) and processing properties.

Direct Vitrification of HLW [30]

 A direct feed option is under consideration to support early start-up and operation of the
WTP HLW vitrification facility. Under this option, the WTP pretreatment facility would be
bypassed and the HLW fed directly to the vitrification facility. Ultrafiltration and caustic
leaching that would otherwise be performed during pretreatment would be either omitted or
replaced by an interim pretreatment function such as in-tank washing and settling. This strategy
was evaluated at VSL by developing new glass and feed formulations for HLW with minimal or
no pretreatment, with focus on the impacts of increased supernate in the HLW. Two candidate
HLW blends were investigated: unwashed HLW solids for AY-102/AP-102 (i.e., sludge from
Hanford Tank AY-102 would be retrieved into Tank AP-102) and singly washed solids from A-
R-AP106 (i.e., all A Farm tanks would be retrieved into Tank AP-106). Glass formulations were
developed and tested on crucible scale for both waste blends, including fourteen AY-102/AP-102
formulations and seven A-R-AP106 formulations, before target glasses were selected to support
subsequent melter testing.

 Because of contributions from the LAW fraction in the feed, both of the selected target
glasses have relatively high total waste loadings. The HLW waste loadings of these glasses could
be marginally increased by extra wash cycles but these were realized at the expense of total
waste loading and the additional costs associated with more extensive washings. Test results
from this study demonstrated that the optimum washing strategy – in terms of minimizing the
number of HLW glass canisters, processing duration and secondary waste generation – varies
depending on the tank composition, and needs to be evaluated on a tank-by-tank basis.

 As seen in Table 2.1, 95 HLW glasses from the four glass formulation studies described
above are incorporated into the HLW database, including 21 CCC samples that are considered

 18

near replicates. Table 2.2 summarizes the compositions of these glasses. A summary of the
property data collected is given in Section 4.

2.2 Development of Test Matrix

 In addition to incorporating new HLW glass data into the existing WTP/ORP database on
a regular basis, earlier aspects of this work have focused on augmenting the database with new
glass formulations. These formulations were statistically designed and developed to bridge the
gaps or fill in the lower-coverage composition regions identified from analyses of the existing
compositional distributions. During previous enhancements, 140 new HLW glasses were
statistically designed before fabrication and characterization to update the database and the HLW
glass property-composition models [22-24]. However, as documented previously, while the new
data improved the coverage and underpinning of the models, there was only limited gain in
model performance as judged by key statistics of regression and model validation [24]. This is
not surprising in view of the very large high-dimensional composition space and the relatively
limited number of new glass formulations, as compared to the size of the combined database
with over 900 glasses. As an attempt to reduce the prediction errors of the property-composition
models, a different approach was adopted in this work to design the augmentation matrix of 40
HLW glass formulations. Instead of filling compositional gaps, the new test matrix was designed
to place HLW formulations in the compositional space where relatively large errors were
projected by the latest HLW glass property-composition models [24]. The models for spinel
crystallization (spinel T1%) and PCT leaching have been found to have the largest errors in both
regression and model validation [24]. These models were therefore used as the bases in the
selection of influential formulations in the present text matrix.

 The HLW test matrix was designed in two stages. In Stage One, a large set (10,000) of
compositional points was generated using JMP® Pro Space Filling Mixture Design [31] to
uniformly cover the design region. The design region was defined compositionally by a list of
lower and upper bounds for the major glass oxides (see Table 2.3). It is seen in Table 2.3 that the
design region mostly overlaps with the compositional ranges of the existing spinel T1% dataset.
Multiple composite linear constraints and selected property constraints were also applied in
defining the design region; they are listed in Table 2.4.

 The 10,000 HLW glass formulations generated in Stage One were screened in Stage Two
to exclude glasses that would not be viable HLW formulations based on model predictions of
their properties. The evaluation properties included PCT-B release, spinel crystallization, and
melt viscosity, all of which were predicted using the property-composition models developed
during Phase 3 of this work [24]. Additionally, a constraint was introduced to limit phosphate
secondary phase formation (see Table 2.4 and Reference [32]). The down selection reduced the
candidate formulations to 1,379 from 10,000. The remaining formulations were then ranked
according to their respective property-composition model prediction errors associated with
PCT-B release and spinel T1%. The top 100 formulations with the largest prediction errors for
PCT-B release and the top 100 for spinel T1% were retained for final consideration, giving a total
of 181 unique formulations.

 19

 The final selection of the test matrix was based on the model prediction errors (for both
PCT-B release and spinel T1%) and their distribution in the composition space. The remaining
181 formulations were surveyed to ensure they did not overlap with existing HLW glasses before
they were partitioned into 40 distinct clusters using the JMP® Pro K Means Cluster platform,
with each cluster consisting of one or more formulation points [31]. This method partitions the
glasses into clusters so as to minimize distances to the cluster centroids. Finally, one formulation
was selected from each of the 40 clusters according to a composite error ranking that consisted of
a sum of studentized relative prediction errors for the PCT-B release and spinel T1% model
response. No replicates were included in the design. Table 2.5 lists the resultant HLW
formulations, which constitute the HLW18 glass series.

 The distribution of the HLW18 formulations is shown in the matrix of two-dimensional
scatter plots in Figure 2.1 (40 glasses only) and Figure 2.2 (together with the existing spinel T1%
dataset). Figure 2.3 displays the frequency distribution of the of the HLW18 glasses with respect
to the 19 design variables. Figure 2.4 provides various binary plots of major oxides in the
HLW18 glasses in comparison to the existing spinel T1% dataset. Although the HLW18 matrix
design was not based on filling compositional gaps, it can be seen in Figure 2.4 that the
distribution of the HLW18 glasses does cover some of the more sparsely populated regions in the
T1% dataset.

 20

 SECTION 3.0
 EXPERIMENTAL METHODS

 The experimental procedures used in the preparation and characterization of the HLW18
glasses discussed in Section 2.2 are presented in this section. The following subsections describe
the preparation of glass batches, crucible glass melting, glass composition analysis, and test
procedures for PCT, T1%, electrical conductivity, viscosity, TCLP, and nepheline formation on
CCC heat treatment.

3.1 Glass Batching and Preparation

 Glass preparation began with a batching sheet that provided information on the required
starting materials and their weights. The information included the chemicals needed,
identification of the chemicals according to vendors and catalog numbers with the associated
purity, and the amounts necessary to produce about 420 g of glass with the target composition.
Chemicals were weighed and batched according to the batching sheets. The information found in
the batching sheets, including actual weights of chemicals used and their associated purities, can
be used to calculate the compositions of the glasses. The batching chemicals were defined such
that the calculated compositions equaled the target compositions of the HLW18 glasses.

 A blender was used to mix and homogenize the starting materials before they were
loaded into platinum-gold crucibles (Pt-Au) that were engraved with individual identification
numbers. The crucible melts were prepared in random order. After batching was completed, the
loaded platinum-gold crucibles were placed inside a Deltech DT-28 (or DT-29) furnace with a
Eurotherm 2404 temperature controller. The glasses were melted for 120 minutes at 1150°C.
Mixing of the melt was accomplished mechanically using a platinum stirrer, beginning 15
minutes after the furnace temperature reached the target and continuing for the next 90 minutes.
The molten glass was poured at the end of the melting period onto a graphite plate to cool.

3.2 Analyses of Glass Compositions

 The primary method used for glass composition analysis was X-Ray Fluorescence (XRF)
spectroscopy on powdered glass samples. A PANalytical Axios mAX-Advanced wavelength
dispersive XRF spectrometer was used for this purpose. The XRF spectrometer was calibrated
over a range of glass compositions using standard reference materials traceable to the National
Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST), as well as waste glasses such as Argonne
National Laboratory – Low Activity Waste Reference Material (ANL-LRM) [33] and Defense
Waste Processing Facility – Environmental Assessment (DWPF-EA) glass [34]. XRF analysis
provides data for most glass components of interest, except lithium and boron.

 21

3.3 Product Consistency Test

 The PCT was conducted using 4 g of crushed glass (100-200 mesh, 75-149 m) placed in
40 ml of test solution (de-ionized water) inside 304L stainless steel vessels. These test conditions
result in a ratio of the glass surface area to the solution volume of about 2000 m-1. The PCTs
were performed at 90ºC for 7 days according to American Society of Testing and Materials
(ASTM) [35], in accordance with the current WTP contract requirement [36, 37]. All tests were
conducted in triplicate, in parallel with the DWPF-EA standard glass included in each test set.
The leachates were sampled after 7 days. One milliliter (ml) of sampled leachate was mixed with
20 ml of 1M HNO3 and the resulting solution was analyzed by Direct Current Plasma-Atomic
emission Spectroscopy (DCP-AES). Another 3 ml of sampled leachate was used for pH
measurement, which was done at room temperature.

 In addition to the leachate concentrations, it is convenient and conventional to also
consider the normalized leachate concentrations. The normalization is performed by dividing the
concentration measured in the leachate for any given component by its fraction in the glass.
Thus, the normalized concentration Ci of element i (in g/L) is calculated from the elemental
concentration ci measured in the leachate (in g/L) as:

 ci
 Ci = , (3.1)
 fi

where fi is the mass fraction of element i in the glass.

 The surface area of the glass sample tested and the volume of leachant used will also
affect the measured leachate concentrations and, therefore, a standard value of their ratio
(2000 m-1) is specified in the PCT method [35]. A further normalization for this effect is often
considered by dividing the normalized concentration by the ratio of the surface area of glass
exposed to the solution volume (S/V, in m-1). The normalized mass loss (in g/m2) is then
obtained from

 Ci
 Li = , (3.2)
 (S /V )

where S/V is the ratio of the glass surface area to the volume of the leachant, which for the
standard PCT is nominally 2000 m-1. Assuming this value of S/V, if Ci is expressed in g/L, one
need only divide by two to obtain Li in g/m2. The Waste Acceptance Product Specifications for
vitrified HLW [36] requires that the normalized mass losses of PCT-B, PCT-Li, and PCT-Na be
below the respective values for the DWPF-EA glass. The nominal values for normalized leachate
concentrations from the DWPF-EA glass are 16.695, 9.565, and 13.346 g/L for PCT-B, PCT-Li,
and PCT-Na, respectively.

 22

3.4 One-Percent Crystal Fraction Temperature (T1%)

 Glass samples (about 3 to 5 grams each) were melted in a covered platinum or platinum
alloy crucible at a temperature of 1200°C for 1 hour to establish a common starting point before
heat treatment for 70 hours at selected temperatures between 700°C and 1150°C. At the end of the
heat-treatment period, the samples were quenched by contacting the crucible with cold water. The
sample was then prepared (ground to -18 mesh) for Scanning Electron Microscopy/Energy
Dispersive X-ray Spectroscopy (SEM/EDS) to determine the volume fraction of crystalline phases
and for identification of the major crystalline phases. The SEM/EDS analysis was performed using
a scanning electron microscope (JEOL Model JSM-5910LV equipped with Oxford Instruments
INCAEnergy 300 system). The crystal fraction (vol%) was generally obtained as the average of
four (up to eight) viewing area counts from the powdered samples. In general, a sufficient number
of heat treatments were performed for each glass to obtain non-zero crystallinity data for at least
three temperatures in order to reasonably constrain the T1% value. In a few cases, however, non-zero
crystallinity data were available for fewer than three temperatures. Effort was also made to bracket
the T1% value so that it could be estimated by interpolation rather than extrapolation.

 Alternatively, the heat-treated samples were analyzed by X-ray diffraction (XRD) using
whole pattern fitting. Powdered samples (-325 mesh) of heat-treated glasses were homogenized
with an internal standard (α-Al2O3) before XRD patterns were obtained with a Thermo-Scientific
X’tra θ-θ XRD system. Diffraction patterns were analyzed using JADE9 (MDI, Inc.) software with
the Rietveld whole pattern fitting module to identify the crystalline phase(s) and calculate the wt%
of each phase. The wt% crystallinity data were converted to vol% with the known densities of the
phases and the measured densities of the HLW glasses.

 The T1% value for each HLW glass was obtained by linear regression of the heat-treatment
temperature (°C) as the dependent variable versus crystal fraction (vol%) as the independent
variable. The choice of vol% as the independent variable, which has larger measurement errors than
temperature, is contrary to the selection that would normally be made for regression. As discussed
during the WTP models development [2], this “inverse regression” approach has the advantage of
facilitating the calculation of the standard deviation of T1% value. The differences in the T1% values
estimated using either choice of independent variable were generally small.

3.5 Electrical Conductivity

 The electrical conductivity (EC) of HLW glasses was determined by measuring the
impedance of the glass melt at temperatures around 950ºC, 1050ºC, 1150ºC and 1250ºC as a
function of alternate current frequency using a calibrated platinum-rhodium electrode probe
attached to a Hewlett-Packard model 4194A or an Agilent 4294A impedance analyzer. The
collected impedance data were analyzed to obtain the direct current EC. The probe (analyzer
along with the crucible to assure that the geometry is replicated) was calibrated and checked
periodically using NIST traceable standard reference materials.

 23

 The current WTP requirement is that glass melt EC be in the range of 0.1 to
0.7 Siemens/cm at 1100ºC to 1200ºC [38].

3.6 Viscosity

 The melt viscosity (η) of each HLW glass was measured using a Brookfield viscometer
with a platinum-rhodium spindle and crucible. The relative torque of a rotating spindle immersed
in molten glass was measured as a function of rotational velocity (revolutions per minute (RPM))
at temperatures around 950ºC, 1050ºC, 1150ºC and 1250ºC. The viscosity of the molten glass
was then calculated from the collected data of torque versus RPM. The equipment was calibrated
using viscosity standard oils and checked periodically using a NIST traceable standard reference
glass.

 Per current WTP requirements, glass melts should satisfy the viscosity limits of 10 poise
to 150 poise at 1100ºC [38].

3.7 Toxicity Characteristic Leaching Procedure (TCLP)

 The TCLP was performed on glass samples according to Environmental Protection
Agency (EPA) SW-846 Method 1311. This test determines the leach resistance of crushed glass
(< 3/8”) in a sodium acetate buffer solution after 18 hours at 22°C with constant end-over-end
agitation. A mass of about 100 g of glass is leached in 2 L of TCLP extraction solution,
according to the method for non-volatiles. The surface area to volume ratio for this test is about
20 m-1, which is about two orders of magnitude lower than that in the PCT. The leachate
concentrations were measured by DCP-AES.

3.8 Nepheline Formation on Canister Centerline Cooling (CCC)

 Glass samples that underwent CCC heat treatment were distinguished from the original
glass samples by adding the extension “CCC” to the sample identifications. The CCC
temperature profile was provided by the WTP project [39]. Table 3.1 reproduces the HLW CCC
temperature profile. As is the case with isothermal heat treatment, the glass samples in platinum
or platinum alloy crucibles were maintained at a “pre-melt” temperature of 1200°C for 1 hour
before initiation of the CCC heat treatment. At the end of the heat treatment, the samples were
recovered and processed for characterization by either SEM/EDS or XRD (see Section 3.4).

 24

 SECTION 4.0
 HLW GLASS CHARACTERIZATION DATA

 As described in Section 2, the present enhancement work incorporates 135 HLW glasses
into the database, including 74 actively designed formulations, 21 CCC near replicates, and 40
statistically designed HLW18 glasses. These 135 glasses are collectively referred to as
ORP-2018 glasses. Table 4.1 lists the ORP-2018 glasses together with the property data
collected for them. Since the major objective of the earlier glass studies was to develop and
identify target glass formulation to support melter tests, glasses that did not comply with any
processing or product quality constraints were not fully characterized. Consequently, only a
subset of the actively designed glasses was characterized for selected properties. Further,
cadmium was not a constituent in any of the waste simulants for these studies and so no TCLP-
Cd release data were collected. Finally, high phosphorus wastes were the subject of two of the
earlier studies and the secondary phase after heat treatment was predominately phosphate; thus
these studies did not supply new spinel T1% data. Overall, only 64 glasses from the earlier studies
have measured data for at least one of the following properties: PCT, electrical conductivity,
viscosity, spinel T1%, and nepheline formation. That number is further reduced to 52 if only
properties predicted by WTP baseline models are included (i.e., excluding nepheline formation).
In contrast, efforts were made to collect data for each of the 40 HLW18 matrix glasses on all
properties (with the exception of two Cd-free glasses, for which TCLP was not performed): PCT,
electrical conductivity, viscosity, spinel T1%, TCLP-Cd releases, and nepheline formation. Data
are available for most of the listed properties for all HLW18 glasses with the primary exception
of spinel T1%, because the major crystalline phase was not spinel in some cases or because
available crystallinity data were insufficient for T1% estimation. Additionally, viscosity data were
unavailable for eight HLW18 glasses because they exhibited non-Newtonian behavior during
measurements (usually indicative of crystallization during the measurement). This section
presents and discusses the results for PCT, spinel T1%, electrical conductivity, viscosity, TCLP-
Cd release and nepheline formation.

 Table 4.2 compares the number of glasses collected in the HLW database according to
the work phases and the properties of interest. With the present addition of 135 glasses, there are
1071 HLW glasses in the database. The number of glasses with property data range from 270 for
TCLP-Cd release to 680 for PCT-boron and PCT-sodium releases.

 Compositions of the HLW18 matrix glasses were analyzed by XRF before measurements
of their properties and are given in Table 4.3.

4.1 Product Consistency Test (PCT) Results

 Table 4.1 shows that 76 of the 135 ORP-2018 glasses have data on PCT-boron (PCT-B),
PCT-sodium (PCT-Na), and PCT-lithium (PCT-Li) releases. Fifteen of these glasses underwent
CCC heat treatment before PCT and ten of them are considered near replicates of the quenched

 25

The Catholic University of America Enhancement of HLW Glass Property-Composition Models
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-19R4480-1, Rev. 0

versions (PCT was not performed for the quenched versions of the other five glasses). Table 4.4
summarizes the PCT-B, PCT-Li, and PCT-Na releases (in ppm) for the ORP-2018 glasses
together with the pH values measured on the leachates. Table 4.4 also gives the normalized PCT
concentrations (g/L) and mass losses (g/m2), as calculated using target mass fractions of boron,
lithium, and sodium in Equations (3.1) and (3.2).

 The normalized PCT-B releases for the ORP-2018 glasses vary between 0.252 g/L and
16.797 g/L, the normalized PCT-Li releases vary between 0.299 g/L and 10.925 g/L, and the
PCT-Na releases vary between 0.242 g/L and 9.244 g/L. The releases are all lower than the
respective limits determined for the benchmark DWPF-EA glass (i.e., 16.70 g/L for PCT-B, 9.57
g/L for PCT-Li, and 13.35 g/L for PCT-Na [36]) with only the following exceptions: HLW18-37
exceeds the PCT-B limit while HLW-HMn-10, HLW-HMn-10CCC, HLW-HMn-13CCC, and
HLW18-12 exceed the PCT-Li limit.

 Figure 4.1 shows the ranges and median values of the normalized PCT-B, PCT-Li, and
PCT-Na releases measured for the 76 ORP-2018 glasses. These results are also compared in
Figure 4.1 with those obtained for the WTP glasses and the previous ORP glasses; the ORP-2018
glasses encompass narrower ranges but have larger median values. Figure 4.2 shows the
distributions of normalized PCT-B, PCT-Li, and PCT-Na releases for the 76 ORP-2018 glasses
and compares them to the distributions of 604 HLW glasses in the existing database. The
distributions again demonstrate the generally higher PCT releases measured for the ORP-2018
glasses. This represents a valuable addition to the database since previous WTP modeling
database contained relatively few glasses with high PCT releases and it was recommended that
additional data with higher releases be collected to enhance the PCT models [2]. However, the
PCT data available for modeling continue to be dominated by low PCT releases, even though it
is desirable for the purpose of model development to include more glasses with high-PCT
releases.

 Figure 4.3 is a scatterplot matrix of the natural logarithms of the PCT-B, PCT-Li, and
PCT-Na normalized releases for the 76 ORP-2018 glasses. It can be seen that both the
statistically designed HLW18 glasses and the actively formulated glasses cover very similar
concentration ranges, although there are more high-alumina glasses in the HLW18 matrix. The
non-linear blending effect of alumina concentration on PCT releases is complex as has been
discussed before [24].

4.2 Spinel One-Percent Crystal Fraction Temperature (T1%) Results

 Heat treatment of the HLW18 glasses was performed between 700ºC and 1150ºC,
typically at temperature intervals of 50ºC. The measured crystal vol% data and major crystalline
phases are given in Table 4.5. Heat treatment data for the other ORP-2018 glasses were reported
previously [27-30]. As expected, spinel was the predominant secondary phase identified in the
heat treated HLW18 samples. However, many other crystalline phases were also observed,
including apatite, calcium phosphate, lithium phosphate, strontium/calcium/sodium phosphate,
nickel oxide, calcium/strontium silicate, nepheline, pyroxene, and zirconium-containing species
(ZrO2 and Na/K/ZrSiO4). For comparison, the actively designed ORP-2018 glasses precipitated

 26

primarily spinel (in high-manganese HLW glasses [28] and high-aluminum/iron glasses
developed for HLW direct feed testing [30]) and phosphate (in glasses developed for processing
high-phosphate HLW [27, 29]). The measured vol% crystallinity for HLW18 glasses covered a
very broad range, from trace to over 40 vol%, the larger values resulting from the formation of
silicate phases such as nepheline and pyroxene. Linear regression of the heat-treatment data in
Table 4.5 provided estimates of T1% values for the ORP-2018 glasses, following the previous
approach and fitting the temperature and vol% data to the equation

                                               T = ao + a1 v,                                     (4.1)

where v is the vol% crystallinity at temperature T (ºC), and ao and a1 are the fitted intercept and
slope, respectively. Table 4.6 presents the fitted intercepts and slopes, together with the
estimated T1% values and the number of regression points. The major crystalline phase(s)
observed at temperatures near T1% are also given in Table 4.6.

 Overall, a T1% value was estimated for 29 actively designed ORP-2018 glasses and for 25
HLW18 glasses. Attempts to estimate T1% for the remaining 15 HLW18 glasses were
complicated by insufficient crystallization data even at low temperatures (e.g., HLW18-03) or
massive formation of another phase such as nepheline (e.g., HLW18-17). In a few cases, large
extrapolation was required such that the fitted T1% value was far outside of the heat-treatment
temperature range (e.g., HLW18-10); it was ultimately decided to exclude those glasses from the
T1% database.

 The T1% values in Table 4.6 span a range of 719.9ºC to 1162.5ºC, with a median of
940.9ºC (mean = 951.5ºC), which can be compared with the recommended WTP processing
requirement of T1% ≤ 950ºC. Figure 4.4 is a distribution histogram of the estimated spinel T1% for
the 54 ORP-2018 glasses.

4.3 Electrical Conductivity Results

 Table 4.1 shows that 69 of the 135 ORP-2018 glasses have data on electrical conductivity
(EC). The combined WTP and ORP database now consists of 594 HLW glasses with EC data
(Table 4.2). The EC versus temperature data for the ORP-2018 glasses are given in Table 4.7.
The measured EC data for HLW18 glasses were fitted using the Vogel-Fulcher equation:

                                     ln (EC, S/cm) = [A/(T - To)] + C ,                           (4.2)

where T is the temperature and A, C, and To are fitting parameters. The fitted EC values at
1150ºC were compared with the values predicted by the Phase 3 global EC model [24] and the
results are shown in Figure 4.5. There is generally good agreement between the fitted and
predicted values with the exception of the EC values for glass HLW18-04, which has a
considerably lower fitted (measured) EC than predicted. Subsequent review of the measurement
process did not reveal any issues and the EC data for HLW18-04 were retained in the database.

 The WTP processing requirement for HLW glass melts is that the EC of the glass melt be
within the range of 0.1 to 0.7 S/cm at temperatures between 1100 and 1200ºC [38]. All of the

 27

The Catholic University of America Enhancement of HLW Glass Property-Composition Models
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-19R4480-1, Rev. 0

actively designed ORP-2018 glasses meet this requirement, but three HLW18 glasses have
measured EC below 0.1 S/cm (HLW18-06, -13 and -31). No ORP-2018 glass exceeds the
recommended upper limit of 0.7 S/cm. The EC versus temperature data in Table 4.7 vary from
0.023 S/cm (HLW18-13 at 977 ºC) to 0.829 S/cm (HLW18-11 at 1265 ºC). The ORP-2018 data
are compared with the EC values from the earlier datasets in Table 4.8, where it is seen that the
present data do not expand the existing range of measured EC.

 Figure 4.6 shows the distribution of temperatures at which EC data were collected for the
ORP-2018 glasses. Figure 4.7 graphically presents the EC data as a function of measurement
temperature. The measurement temperatures in Figure 4.6 are all higher than the four nominal
temperatures of 950ºC, 1000ºC, 1150ºC, and 1250ºC. Deviations from the nominal temperatures,
however, are moderate (4ºC to 36ºC) and are not expected to affect the suitability of the data for
model development [2].

4.4 Viscosity Results

 Of the 114 ORP-2018 (excluding CCC near replicates) glasses described in Section 2, 69
were characterized for melt viscosity, including all 40 HLW18 matrix glasses. Many of the
HLW18 glasses, however, displayed unstable and non-Newtonian behavior during viscosity
measurements, especially at low temperatures when crystallization was apparent. As a result,
viscosity data could be collected only at fewer than four temperatures for the following glasses:
HLW18-05, -09, -17, -20, -24, -26, -27, and -37). Viscosity data from these eight glasses were
excluded from the modeling database since glasses with fewer than four data points were not
used in previous model developments [2, 22-24]. As a result, viscosity data for 61 ORP-2018
glasses were incorporated into the database (see Table 4.1), bringing the total to 559 glasses, as
shown in Table 4.2. Table 4.9 gives the viscosity versus temperature data for the 69 ORP-2018
glasses, including for eight glasses listed above that show non-Newtonian behavior.

 The measured viscosity data for the HLW18 glasses were fitted using the Vogel-Fulcher
equation:

                                     ln (viscosity, P) = [A/(T - To)] + C ,                        (4.3)

where T is the temperature and A, C, and To are fitting parameters. The fitted viscosity values at
1150ºC were compared with the values predicted by the Phase 3 global viscosity model [24] and
the results are shown in Figure 4.8. The comparison identifies an outlier, HLW18-06, which has
a much larger fitted (measured) viscosity than predicted. Subsequent review of the glass
composition and measurement process did not reveal any issues; a repeated measurement also
produced similar data. For example, the initial measurement gave a viscosity value of 147.32 P at
1159ºC while the repeated measurement gave 142.09 P at the same temperature. The viscosity
data for HLW18-06 were subsequently retained in the database. In addition, this glass showed
unstable behavior below 1000ºC and so viscosity measurement was made at 1007ºC instead of
the nominal 950ºC.

 The recommended viscosity limit for processing HLW glass melt at WTP is 10 P to
150 P at 1100ºC [38]. All of the actively designed ORP-2018 glasses meet this requirement

 28

except HLW-HP-03 (with an interpolated viscosity at 1100ºC of 194 P) while the statistically
designed HLW18-06 and -07 glasses also exceed the recommended upper limit. There are no
ORP-2018 glasses with viscosities below the lower processing limit of 10P at 1100ºC. The
viscosity versus temperature data in Table 4.9 range between 5.09 P (HLW18-34 at 1257ºC) and
1872.36 P (HLW18-07 at 956ºC). These values are compared in Table 4.10 with the viscosity
data collected for earlier datasets. The ORP-2018 viscosity data do not expand the existing data
range.

 Figure 4.9 shows the distribution of temperatures at which viscosity data were collected
for the ORP-2018 glasses. Figure 4.10 graphically presents the viscosity data as a function of
measurement temperature. Compared to EC measurements, the viscosity measurement
temperatures in Figure 4.9 are closer to the four nominal temperatures of 950ºC, 1050ºC,
1150ºC, and 1250ºC, with the notable exception of HLW18-06 (see above). Deviations from the
nominal temperatures are typically less than 15ºC and are not expected to affect the suitability of
the data for model development [2].

4.5 TCLP-Cd Release Results

 Of the 114 ORP-2018 (excluding CCC near replicates) glasses described in Section 2,
only the statistically designed HLW18 glasses contain CdO as a glass component. In addition,
HLW18-38 and -40 were designed to include 0 wt% CdO. Consequently, only 38 ORP-2018
glasses have TCLP Cd-release data. This represents a moderate increase in the number of HLW
glasses with TCLP Cd-release data, bringing the total to 270 (see Table 4.2). The CdO
concentrations in the 38 HLW18 glasses span a narrower range when compared to earlier designs
[24], varying between 0.078 wt% to 0.487 wt%. For comparison, the WTP statistical design
included 100 glasses with CdO ranging from 0.05 wt% to 1.60 wt%. Table 4.11 gives the
measured TCLP Cd-release data (cCd, mg/L) for these glasses; it also gives the normalized
Cd-release data (rCd, g/L), which were calculated according to:

 cCd (mg / L)
               rCd ( g / L) =                                                                    ,    (4.4)
                                [1000 (mg / g )][mCdO (g CdO / g glass )][0.8754 (g Cd / g CdO)]

where mCdO is the mass fraction of CdO in glass. The normalized release is calculated as the Cd
concentration in the TCLP leachate (mg of Cd/L) divided by the mass fraction of Cd in glass
(mg of Cd/g of glass) and therefore has a unit of (g of glass/L). Both cCd and rCd were considered
in developing the different TCLP-Cd release models.

 The 38 glasses in Table 4.11 have TCLP Cd-releases that range from 0.03 mg/L to
10.60 mg/L for the unnormalized data and 0.031 g/L to 4.114 g/L for the normalized data. Figure
4.11 relates the unnormalized TCLP-Cd releases with the normalized releases and shows that
HLW18-16 is a substantial outlier. Review of glass fabrication and test procedures did not
uncover any issues but that unusually high concentrations of other metals (e.g., Cr and Ni) and
glass forming components (e.g., B and Si) were also found in the TCLP leachate. Exclusion of
HLW18-16 from the dataset reduced the maximum response value to 3.02 mg/L (unnormalized
Cd release) and 0.847 g/L (normalized Cd release). The TCLP-Cd releases in Table 4.11 are

 29

relatively high compared to existing data, including 14 that exceed the delisting limit of
0.48 mg/L.

4.6 Nepheline Formation Results

 As discussed above, a property-composition model to predict and control nepheline
formation during HLW vitrification was not included in the development of WTP baseline
models [2]. Instead of developing a baseline nepheline model, a constraint based on normalized
silica concentration (the nepheline discriminator [25]) was used to address nepheline formation
during canister cooling. Work on the development of new nepheline models, however, has been
performed at VSL under a separate ORP task. Statistical approaches that are suited for
categorical data (e.g., nepheline present/absent) have been investigated with the support of the
WTP/ORP dataset and other glass data [40, 41]. Further development and enhancement of the
nepheline prediction models should continue to improve the misclassification rate of nepheline
formation.

 The present work collected nepheline formation data for 78 ORP-2018 glasses, including
all 40 glasses from the HLW18 series (Table 4.1). Table 4.12 gives the crystallinity data upon
CCC heat treatment, including total vol% crystallinity estimates, identity of the major crystalline
phase(s) and, when applicable and available, vol% of nepheline. Of the 78 glasses listed in table
4.12, 20 precipitated nepheline after CCC heat treatment. Small amounts (< 0.5 vol%) of
nepheline were observed in CCC samples of another three glasses (HLW-HPA-08,
HLW-HPA-09, and HLW18-35). In addition to nepheline, the primary crystalline phases found
in actively designed glasses were phosphate (lithium phosphate or Na/Ca phosphate) and spinel.
For the statistically designed HLW18 glasses, spinel was the most prevalent phase.

 30

 SECTION 5.0
 HLW GLASS PROPERTY-COMPOSITION MODELS

 As stated in Section 1, test objectives of the present work include assessment of the HLW
glass property-composition models with the expanded datasets and refinement of the property-
composition models. This section discusses updating and assessing the models with the
combined WTP/ORP database to predict various properties of HLW glasses. Previously, models
were developed for PCT-B, PCT-Li, PCT-Na, spinel T1%, melt viscosity, melt electrical
conductivity, and Cd-TCLP [22-24]; models for nepheline formation were not addressed because
that is the subject of a separate task [40, 41].

 Updates of the HLW property-composition models are required to take advantage of the
expanded WTP/ORP dataset, which incorporated 135 additional glasses and provided new data
for the glass properties listed above (Tables 4.1 and 4.2). The present model updates followed an
approach similar to that from previous modeling efforts, which explored both global and local
models. The WTP baseline models are all global models since each model uses a single model
form over the entire range of compositions of interest. As the HLW database continues to
expand, the relationship between selected properties and glass compositions may become too
complex to be represented by a single mathematical model form, resulting in worsened model
performance and less accurate property prediction. Earlier modeling efforts, therefore, also
developed local models that covered smaller compositional regions in order to improve modeling
and prediction performances [22-24]. Local models were developed for two compositional
subsets that resulted from dividing the combined WTP/ORP database into a low alumina region
(≤ 13.1 wt% Al2O3) and a high alumina region (> 13.1 wt% Al2O3). As described above, this
choice of division was made over other choices because: (i) while the WTP glasses were developed
primarily for iron-limited wastes, many of the ORP glasses were formulated for the aluminum and
aluminum/sodium-limited wastes, which represent a large fraction of the overall waste inventory;
(ii) the maximum component limit for Al2O3 in the WTP baseline validity region is 13.1 wt%;
and (iii) the design feature for high-alumina augmentation during the earlier enhancement phases
limits the Al2O3 content to a minimum of 13.1 wt%. Updates to both the global and the two local
models are discussed below. Of the newly added 135 glasses in the database, 79 are glasses with
≤ 13.1 wt% Al2O3 (including 27 HLW18 glasses and 12 near replicates) while 56 are glasses
with > 13.1 wt% Al2O3 (including 13 HLW18 glasses and 9 near replicates).

 In addition to local models, earlier modeling efforts also investigated models of different
forms. Complicated non-linear blending effects of glass components are known to exist over the
very broad range of WTP HLW glass compositions. For example, alumina has been reported to
have a non-linear effect on PCT responses, improving glass durability with respect to PCT at low
concentrations, but leveling off at intermediate concentrations before degrading glass durability
at the highest concentrations [41-45]. Cubic polynomial models therefore have been examined to
determine if they are better suited to fit the complicated relationships between various properties
and glass compositions. During development of the WTP baseline models, partial cubic mixture

 31

(PCM) models were investigated for the PCT releases but the resulting models provided only
marginally better modeling performance while the validation performance was inferior when
compared to the partial quadratic mixture (PQM) models [2]. The PQM models were, therefore,
recommended as the baseline instead of the PCM models. Similarly, when applied to the much
broader compositional range of the WTP/ORP combined database, cubic models that were
developed during Phase 3 enhancement also showed considerably worse validation statistics than
those for the PQM models [24]. Since the cubic model forms did not provide any advantage or
improvement over the PQM models, they were not pursued further in this work.

 One objective of this work is to improve the performance of the HLW property-
composition models. Evaluations of model performance are primarily based on goodness-of-fit
statistics and how well the models predict property values for glasses not used in model fitting.
Consequently, design of the HLW18 augmentation matrix focused on reducing model prediction
errors instead of providing a more even coverage in glass compositions, as discussed in Section
2.2. While it is preferred to have a separate dataset not used in modeling available for validation
(data-partitioning), it may be difficult to achieve in practice because of limited data availability
and the desire to fit the models with all appropriate data. In earlier work [22-23], only k-fold
cross-validation was used for model validation. In k-fold validation, the statistical analysis
software JMP® randomly splits the dataset into k subsets. Each of the subsets is used to validate
the model obtained by fitting the rest of the data, giving a total of k models and validations.
Similar to data-splitting, which was the primary validation method during WTP baseline models
development [2], k-fold validation allows the maximum use of available data while maintaining a
validation dataset. Data-partitioning was used as a secondary validation approach in the
development of the WTP baseline and Phase 3 models [2, 24]. The present work made use of
both k-fold and data-partitioning validation for global models. For refinement of local models,
the data-partitioning method was not followed because of the more limited availability of data
after the splitting the glasses into two groups according to alumina concentration. Moreover, in
order to facilitate comparison of validation performance, the same validation data subset from
Phase 3 was retained and used in the present validation of the global models. The WTP
validation data subset was selected from a much smaller compositional space than that of the
combined WTP/ORP database and therefore is not suited for validating the updated models (e.g.,
no glasses with > 13.1 wt% Al2O3 are in the WTP validation dataset).

 Section 4 summarizes the data collected on ORP-2018 glasses for PCT, spinel T1%,
electrical conductivity, viscosity, TCLP-Cd release, and nepheline formation. Table 5.1 provides
a summary of the number of HLW glasses available to support model updates for each of the
properties of interest, including division of the glasses according to the alumina concentrations
into Group 1 (Al2O3 ≤ 13.11 wt%) and Group 2 (Al2O3 > 13.1 wt%). It can be seen that Group 1
glasses continue to outnumber Group 2 glasses by a significant margin.

 Results for model updates and enhancements using the expanded WTP/ORP database are
presented and discussed in the following subsections. Summaries of modeling parameters, fit
statistics, and validation statistics of the various models are compiled in each subsection.
Predicted versus measured (PvM) property plots graphically assess the models by showing how
well the predicted values compared to the measured values; PvM property plots are presented for
the glasses in the model development set and, in the case of data-partitioning validation, also for

 32

the glasses in the validation subset. Finally, key goodness-of-fit and validation statistics of the
updated models are compared with and assessed against those from previous model development
work, including the WTP baseline.

 The statistical terms that are used in modeling are described in Appendix C of the WTP
baseline model report [2]. They are reproduced below for convenience.

 The following notation is used in the subsequent descriptions and definitions:

 n = the number of data points used to fit the model,

 p = the number of parameters (coefficients) in the model form estimated via regression on
 the data,

 yi = the measured property value (or, frequently, the natural logarithm of the measured
 property value) for the ith data point,

 ŷi = the predicted property value (or, frequently, the natural logarithm of the predicted
 property value) for the ith data point using the model fitted to all n data points,

 y = the average (mean) of the n measured property values (or, frequently, the average of
 the natural logarithm of the measured property values).

 The residual sum of squares (RSS), which would equal zero if the model was perfect (i.e.,
for all n data points, the predicted property value coincides perfectly with the measured value),
can be calculated using Equation 5.1. The RSS can be compared to the total variability in the
regression model, or the total sum of squares (TSS) expressed as the squared difference between
each measured property value yi and the average y :

 Generally R2 statistics take values between 0 and 1. Negative values of R2 estimated on
the validation set or R2Adjusted reflect a poor fitting model, a model that contains more terms than
needed to fit the data, or a model fitted to data with one or more very influential data points.
More than a minor difference between R2 and R2Adjusted indicates that the model may contain
more terms than needed to achieve the same goodness of fit.

 If the fitted model is adequate, the root mean square error (RMSE) provides an estimate
of the standard deviation associated with melting glasses and measuring the associated property
values being modeled. RMSE is therefore expected to be comparable to the measured glass
preparation and property measurement standard deviation assessed from replicates testing. The
statistic RMSE is included as a standard output in the JMP® software (SAS Institute, Version
10.0) used in modeling, and is given by:

 Optimal prediction yields R2Predict closest to 1 but a poor predictive model can yield

 The statistical lack-of-fit (LOF) test checks whether the differences between measured
and predicted property values from a fitted model are larger than expected based on the
experimental and measurement uncertainty in the data. If the predicted versus measured
differences are larger than data uncertainty at a high enough statistical confidence (e.g., greater
than 90%), the model is said to have a statistically significant LOF. Replicate data points
containing all applicable sources of experimental and measurement uncertainty are required to
perform statistical LOF tests.

This process is conducted using a LOF F-test given by:

 The scatter among the replicates of the measured property (multiple yi for a single xi) is
calculated to assess the scatter of points around the best fit curve. The scatter around the
replicates is pooled into the sum of squares of pure error (SSPE). The sum of squares error (SSE)
is calculated for all of the model data points. The degree of freedom for SSE is n−p; and the
degree of freedom for pure error is given by f =  (mk − 1) , where mk is the number of replicate

data points in the kth replicate set, k = 1, 2, …, K.

 The p-value of the F-test (F probability distribution) is used to assess whether the fitted
model has a statistically significant LOF for the modeling dataset. As the LOF p-value becomes
smaller, the indication that the model is inadequate becomes greater. A threshold LOF p-value of
0.05 is traditionally used to assess whether the fitted model has a statistically significant LOF

 35

(significant at the 95% confidence level) for the modeling dataset and that a different model
might be required.

5.1 PCT-B, PCT-Li, and PCT-Na Models

 5.1.1 PCT Dataset

 Table 5.1 shows that the combined WTP/ORP database contains 676 glasses with data on
PCT boron (PCT-B) releases available for model development. For PCT lithium (PCT-Li) and
PCT sodium releases, the numbers are 634 and 678, respectively. A compositional review of
these glasses revealed one extreme outlier with 15.25 wt% K2O (HLW-E-Es-10), which is
substantially higher than the upper K2O limit of 8.99 wt% found in the remainder of the glasses.
Since K2O is expected to have significant impact on PCT releases, it was decided to remove
HLW-E-Es-10 before modeling.

 As discussed above, the present work made use of both k-fold and data-partitioning
validation for global model development. Further, in order to facilitate comparison of model
prediction performances, the validation subsets were retained from Phase 3 with no new
additions. Consequently, all ORP-2018 glasses were used in model fitting while the number of
validation glasses remained at 136 for PCT-B, 124 for PCT-Li, and 137 for PCT-Na. The present
global modeling subset consisted of all the modeling glasses from the data-partitioning validation
approach during development of the WTP baseline models and all the subsequent statistically
designed augmentation matrices. It also included actively designed glasses that were selected
either to provide more even coverage, or randomly. Finally, the glasses HLW-HSr-3, -7, and -9
were removed from the modeling set because the CCC versions of these glasses were included in
the data-partitioning validation set. As a result, the final modeling datasets contained 672 glasses
for PCT-B, 630 glasses for PCT-Li, and 674 glasses for PCT-Na. Glasses that constituted the
validation subset for data-partitioning have been identified in an earlier report [24].

 5.1.2 Updates of PCT Models

 Four models were developed for the PCT release of each of the three elements (i.e., B, Li,
and Na) during Phase 3 [24], including two global models and two local models. The present
updates considered only the global PQM models and the local models. The global PCM models
were not investigated because they did not offer any significant improvements or advantages
over the PQM models [24]. Updates of the global models employed the expanded modeling
subset and their validation was performed using both data-partitioning with a separate validation
subset and k-fold cross-validation. Because data availability became more limited after division
into Group 1 and Group 2, development of the Local 1 (Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt%) and Local 2 (Al2O3 >
13.1 wt%) models made use of all available data in modeling; data-partition validation was not
performed.

 Fitting of the updated models followed closely the earlier development approach and
employed the Phase 3 models as starting points [24]. The Phase 3 global and local models are
PQM models with the response being the natural logarithm of PCT normalized releases. Natural

 36

The Catholic University of America Enhancement of HLW Glass Property-Composition Models
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-19R4480-1, Rev. 0

logarithmic transformation was also used in WTP baseline models development and its
advantages have been described in Reference [2]. Unlike the WTP baseline models, which
employed bias-corrections to account for under-predictions in PCT releases above certain cutoff
values, the Phase 3 models and present updates included no bias-correction and have the
following model form:

 q q q −1 q 
 ln( C B , C Li or C Na ) =  bi xi + Selected   bii xi2 +   bij xi x j  , (5.9)
                                           i =1               i =1           i j         

where ln(CB), ln(CLi), and ln(CNa) are the natural logarithms of normalized PCT-B, PCT-Li, and
PCT-Na, respectively, the xi (i = 1, 2, …, q) is the normalized mass fraction of the q glass oxides
such that the sum of all oxides equals to 1, while bi, bii and bij are coefficients to be estimated.
Modeling began by fitting the PCT data to a first-order model with no quadratic terms. The
fitting results determined the selection of linear modeling terms in the following manner. The
99%-confidence intervals of the model parameter estimates for the linear terms were compared
against the mean response. If the mean response fell within the confidence levels, it was an
indication that the property was not significantly affected by the model term [46, 47], which was
then eliminated from the model. This approach was similar to that adopted in the development of
LAW property-composition models at VSL [48]. Components that were determined to be of
insufficient significance were included in “Others”. The nonlinear terms in the components of
the PQM model were then evaluated using JMP® Pro 14 (SAS Institute Inc.) Response Surface
methodologies1 on the full dataset. The Response Surface (RS) method was employed in order to
identify both the significant cross-product and squared terms. Stepwise regression allowed
selection of the significant nonlinear terms based on the p-values calculated in the Student
t-tests2. The regression continued until all nonlinear terms in the model had p-values lower than
0.05.

Updated PCT-B Models

 The updated global model for ln (PCT-B) release developed with 536 HLW glasses is a
29-term PQM model, with 11 linear and 16 non-linear terms. Table 5.2 presents the coefficients
of the model, coefficient standard deviations, p-values for the quadratic terms, and performance

1
  JMP offers two Response Surface Methodologies providing methods of augmenting a linear mixture model with
quadratic terms or other nonlinear blending terms. The Mixture Response Surface (MRS) model suppresses the
intercept, includes all the linear main-effect terms, excludes all the squared terms (such as X1×X1), but includes all
cross terms (such as X1×X2). The Response Surface (RS) is different from MRS by including all squared terms
(X1×X1).
2
  The t-test was used in a stepwise regression process to identify important cross-terms in a model while linear terms
were selected on the basis of their confidence intervals. The mixed direction (alternating forward and backward
steps) approach was selected. The t-test computes a t-statistic equal to a model coefficient divided by its standard
deviation. The t-statistic is then compared to the t probability distribution to determine the probability of getting a
t-statistic at least as large as a pre-determined limit. The resulting probability is referred to as a p-value and
represents the probability of incorrectly deciding a coefficient is significantly different than zero. JMP outputs
estimated model coefficients, coefficients standard errors, t-statistics, and p-values. The smaller the p-value the
stronger is the evidence that the coefficient is significantly different from zero, and that the corresponding model
term is needed. To avoid running models with too many potentially unnecessary terms, JMP evaluates p-values for
the coefficients in a “full” model, and the model term whose coefficient is statistically insignificant is not included.

 37

statistics for the model fitting and validation. Table 5.2 also includes the Phase 3 global model
for comparison. The Phase 3 model was developed using 464 glasses and contained two more
non-linear terms.

 The goodness-of-fit statistics for the updated global model in Table 5.2 are comparable to
or slightly worse than those for the Phase 3 model. As was found in previous modeling efforts
[23, 24], large differences in fit statistics are not expected because the number of new glasses
added to the dataset is relatively modest. When applied to the identical validation subset of 136
glasses, the updated model performed better than the Phase 3 model and gave improved
predictions in both relative (R2v = 0.675 vs. 0.621) and absolute (RMSEv = 0.473 v. 0.511) terms.
The data-partitioning validation performance, however, is poorer when compared to the k-fold
validation R2 of 0.780, which can be partly attributed to the non-representative partitioning of the
validation glasses. Figures 5.1 and 5.2 display, respectively, the PvM plots for 536 modeling
glasses and 136 validation glasses for the updated global PCT-B model. The tendency of the
updated model to (i) over-predict PCT-B releases up to about -0.5 ln (g/l) and (ii) under-predict
PCT-B releases above 1.0 ln (g/l) is apparent in Figure 5.2. Similar observations were made
during previous model development work [2, 24]. Thus, the recommended WTP baseline PCT-B
model included a bias-correction term to rectify the under-predictions. The use of bias-correction
was not investigated in this work.

 Developed with 481 Group 1 glasses (Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt%), the updated Local 1 PCT-B
model includes 13 linear and 17 non-linear terms. Table 5.3 summarizes and compares the Phase
3 and updated Local 1 PCT-B models. It can be seen that both sets of modeling and k-fold
validation R2 statistics are very similar. They are also very comparable to those found for the
updated global model (Table 5.2), which covers a much broader compositional region. Figure 5.3
shows the PvM plot for the updated Local 1 PCT-B model.

 The Local 2 (Al2O3 > 13.1 wt%) PCT-B model was revised with an expanded dataset of
191 Group 2 glasses, which represent only a modest increase of 25 glasses over Phase 3. Table
5.4 gives the updated Local 2 PCT-B model together with the Phase 3 Local 2 model for
comparison. The updated model has the same number of terms (8 linear and 9 non-linear) as the
Phase 3 model but the fit and k-fold validation statistics are slightly worse. The PvM plot for the
updated Local 2 PCT-B model is shown in Figure 5.4.

 The major model evaluation and validation statistics for selected global, Local 1, and
Local 2 PCT-B models are summarized in Table 5.5. These statistics are the results of model
developments conducted in several phases over a period of more than 10 years. The HLW
database used to develop these models has grown from 264 glasses (for the WTP baseline
model) to the present 672 glasses, while the compositional space covered has been greatly
expanded. The statistics in Table 5.5 are based on model developments that (i) employed datasets
of different sizes and compositional ranges, (ii) explored global approach versus local approach,
and (iii) performed validation by data-splitting (k-fold validation) and data-partitioning. Table
5.5 shows that, in spite of the much larger compositional space covered, both the Phase 3 and
updated global modes have fit statistics similar to those of the WTP baseline model. The updated
global model performed the best in prediction, giving better validation R2 than the Phase 3 global
model and the WTP baseline model (the updated RMSEv is slightly larger than the baseline

 38

RMSEv but the updated validation dataset includes more glasses with larger PCT-B releases than
the WTP validation dataset). The validation subsets of separate HLW glasses were identical for
the Phase 3 and updated models, while the WTP validation subset was smaller and contained no
glasses with > 13.1 wt% Al2O3. Figure 5.5 graphically compares the adjusted R2 statistics for the
three global PCT-B models; the adjusted R2 statistics are more suited for comparison than the R2
statistics over models with different number of parameters and modeling glasses by including the
degrees of freedom in the calculation.

 No WTP baseline local models were developed for PCT-B and comparisons are made in
Table 5.5 only for local models developed with the combined WTP/ORP database. The fit
statistics for the Local 1 PCT-B models do not show large variations, as expected from the
relatively modest increase in the modeling dataset during each enhancement phase, although the
updated model is marginally better than the other Local 1 models. The Local 2 models, on the
other hand, clearly benefited from the addition of new data for Group 2 glasses (from 71 during
Phase 1 to 191 for the present work). The updated Local 2 model has slightly worse summary
statistics than the Phase 3 model but they are all better than the corresponding statistics for any
of the global models, suggesting that development of local models may be better suited to
capture the PCT-B dependence on glass compositions over the high-alumina region of interest.
Figure 5.6 compares the adjusted R2 statistics for the local PCT-B models developed to date.

Updated PCT-Li Models

 The updated global model for ln (PCT-Li) release developed with 506 HLW glasses is a
31-term PQM model, with 11 linear and 20 non-linear terms. Table 5.6 presents the coefficients
of the model, coefficient standard deviations, p-values for the quadratic terms, and performance
statistics for model fitting and validation. Table 5.6 also includes the Phase 3 global model for
comparison. The Phase 3 model was developed using 434 glasses and contained 25 terms.

 The goodness-of-fit statistics for the updated global model in Table 5.6 are similar to or
slightly better than those for the Phase 3 model. Both models have statistically significant LOF.
As was found in previous modeling efforts [23, 24], large differences in fit statistics are not
expected because the number of new additions to the dataset is relatively modest. When applied
to the identical validation subset of 124 glasses, the updated model performed better than the
Phase 3 model and gave slightly improved predictions in both relative (R2v = 0.520 vs. 0.507)
and absolute (RMSEv = 0.436 v. 0.442) terms. The data-partitioning validation performance,
however, is poorer when compared to the k-fold validation R2 of 0.759, which can be partly
attributed to the non-representative partitioning of the validation glasses since they were not
statistically designed. Figures 5.7 and 5.8 display, respectively, the PvM plot for 506 modeling
glasses and 124 validation glasses for the updated PCT-Li model. Figure 5.8 shows that the
updated model still has a tendency to (i) over-predict PCT-Li releases up to about -0.5 ln (g/l)
and (ii) under-predict PCT-Li releases above 1.0 ln (g/l). Similar observations were made during
previous model development [2, 24]. Thus, the recommended WTP baseline PCT-Li model
included a bias-correction term to rectify the under-predictions. The use of bias-correction was
not investigated in this work.
