5.2 Spinel T1% Models

 5.2.1 Spinel T1% Dataset

 The total number of HLW glasses available for spinel T1% modeling before the present
enhancement work was 442 [24]. The newly collected data described in Section 2 expanded this
number to 496, as summarized in Table 5.1. The present work contributed 40 Group 1 (Al2O3
≤ 13.1 wt%) and 14 Group 2 (Al2O3 > 13.1 wt%) glasses to the spinel T1% database, thus
continuing the trend where there are significantly more Group 1 glasses overall available for
modeling. Prior to the start of model development, the spinel T1% data were reviewed in order to
identify glasses with outlying compositions. The glass HLW-HMn-10, which was actively
formulated for high-manganese HLW [28], was found to have over 10 wt% K2O, which is
substantially higher than the upper K2O limit of 8.46 wt% found in the remainder of the glasses.
That glass was subsequently removed from the modeling dataset. Furthermore, spinel T1% values
of two other glasses (HLW-E-ANa-22 and HWBi4098-9) were found to be estimated through
large extrapolation of crystallinity data that were far away from 1 vol%. These glasses were also
removed, resulting in a final modeling dataset of 493 glasses, including 341 Group 1 and 152
Group 2 glasses.

 As discussed above, the present work made use of both k-fold and data-partitioning
validation in updating the global model. Further, in order to facilitate comparison of model
prediction performance, the validation subset from Phase 3 was retained with no new additions.
Consequently, all ORP-2018 glasses were used in model fitting while the number of validation
glasses remained at 83. Glasses that constituted the validation subset for data-partitioning have
been identified in an earlier report [24].

 42

The Catholic University of America Enhancement of HLW Glass Property-Composition Models
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-19R4480-1, Rev. 0

 5.2.2 Updated Spinel T1% Models

 Four Phase 3 models were developed for prediction of spinel T1%, including two global
models and two local models [24]. The present work provided updates only for the global PQM
model and the two local models; the global PCM model was not considered, as discussed above.
In addition, the updates only considered the same model form, which was given by

 q
 q q −1 q
 
 Spinel T1% =  ai xi + Selected  aii xi2 + aij xi x j  , (5.10)
                                    i =1                i =1      i  j         

where xi (i = 1, 2, …, q) is the normalized mass fractions of the q glass oxides such that their sum
equals to 1, while ai, aii, aij, and bi are coefficients to be estimated, and T1% is in °C. The
modeling approach was similar to that used for PCT model development, where comparison of
the mean response value (961.4°C for global model, 946.1°C for Local 1 model, and 992.0°C for
Local 2 model) against the 99%-confidence intervals of the model parameters was used to select
the model linear terms. Components that were determined to be of insufficient significance were
included in “Others”. The nonlinear terms in the components of the PQM model were then
evaluated using JMP® RS method to identify the significant cross-product and squared terms.
Stepwise regression allowed selection of the significant non-linear terms based on the calculated
p-values. Model reduction continued with the regression until all non-linear terms had p-values
lower than 0.005.

 Table 5.14 gives the updated global spinel T1% model, listing the coefficients, their
standard deviations, p-values for the non-linear terms, and summary statistics for fitting 410
glasses and validating the data-partition set of 83 glasses. The updated model is a 26-term PQM
model with 15 linear and 11 non-linear terms. Table 5.14 also includes the Phase 3 global model
for comparison. The Phase 3 model was developed using 359 glasses and contained four fewer
terms. The goodness-of-fit statistics for the updated global spinel T1% model are somewhat worse
than those for the Phase 3 model. However, when applied to the identical validation subset of 83
glasses, the updated model performed better than the Phase 3 model and gave improved
predictions in both relative (R2v = 0.687 vs. 0.635) and absolute (RMSEv = 80.79 v. 87.24) terms.
The data-partitioning validation R2 of the updated model has the same value as the k-fold
validation R2, indicating that the model predicts equally well for separate validation glasses as
for glasses used in model development. Figures 5.15 and 5.16 show, respectively, the PvM plots
for 410 modeling glasses and 83 validation glasses for the updated global spinel T1% model.

 Developed with 341 Group 1 glasses (Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt%), the updated Local 1 spinel T1%
model includes 16 linear and 13 non-linear terms. Table 5.15 summarizes and compares the
Phase 3 and updated Local 1 spinel T1% models. As is the case with the global models, the
summary statistics of the updated Local 1 model are slightly worse than those for the Phase 3
Local 1 model, which can be expected from the sizeable overlap in the modeling datasets. Both
models have significant LOF. Figure 5.17 shows the PvM plot for the updated Local 1 spinel T1%
model.

 43

 The Local 2 (Al2O3 > 13.1 wt%) spinel T1% model was revised with an expanded dataset
of 152 Group 2 glasses, which represent a rather modest increase of only 13 glasses over Phase
3. Table 5.16 gives the updated Local 2 spinel T1% model together with the Phase 3 Local 2
model for comparison. As expected from the relatively small expansion of the dataset, both
Local 2 models in Table 5.16 have very comparable fit and k-fold validation statistics, although
the updated model has five more terms, including the linear terms of MnO and ZrO2. The PvM
plot for the updated Local 2 spinel T1% model is shown in Figure 5.18.

 The major model evaluation and validation statistics for selected global, Local 1, and
Local 2 spinel T1% models are summarized in Table 5.17. These statistics are the results of model
developments conducted in several phases over a period of more than 10 years. The HLW
database used to develop these models has grown from 168 glasses (for the WTP baseline
model) to the present 493 glasses, while the compositional space covered has been greatly
expanded. The statistics in Table 5.17 are based on model developments that (i) employed
datasets of different sizes and compositional ranges, (ii) explored global approach versus local
approach, and (iii) performed validation by data-splitting (k-fold validation) and data-
partitioning. Table 5.17 shows that, as the compositional space enlarged, both the Phase 3 and
updated global modes have noticeably poorer modeling and validation performances than the
WTP baseline model. For example, while the updated model improved upon the Phase 3 model
in validation performance, its validation statistics (R2v = 0.687 and RMSEv = 80.79) are
considerably worse than those of the baseline model (R2v = 0.881 and RMSEv = 57.99).

 No WTP baseline local models were developed for spinel T1% and comparisons are made
in Table 5.17 only for local models developed with the combined WTP/ORP database. The fit
and validation statistics for the updated Local 1 model are both slightly worse than for earlier
Local 1 models, although they compare favorably with those from the updated global model. The
Local 2 models, in contrast, continue to benefit from the addition of new data for Group 2
glasses (from 92 during Phase 1 to 152 for the present work). Not only does the updated Local 2
model improve upon the Phase 3 Local 2 model performance, it also has better fit statistics than
all the global models in Table 5.17. The comparison in Table 5.17 clearly suggests that local
modeling approaches would be better than a global approach to capture the T1% dependence on
glass compositions over the very large region of interest. Figure 5.19 graphically compares the
adjusted R2 statistics for the various spinel T1% models developed to date; the adjusted R2
statistics are more suited for comparison than the R2 statistics over models with different number
of parameters and modeling glasses by including the degrees of freedom in the calculation.

5.3 Electrical Conductivity Model

 5.3.1 Electrical Conductivity Dataset

 Sections 2 and 4 describe respectively the ORP-2018 glasses and collection of their EC
data. Table 4.1 shows that 69 ORP-2018 glasses have EC data, including all 40 statistically
designed HLW18 glasses. Table 5.1 provides a summary of the number of HLW glasses
available for modeling: the total number of HLW glasses available for EC modeling is now 586
(i.e., all glasses with data at four different temperatures) and 2344 data points, including 402

 44

The Catholic University of America Enhancement of HLW Glass Property-Composition Models
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-19R4480-1, Rev. 0

Group 1 glasses and 184 Group 2 glasses. A review of the glasses with EC data revealed two
glasses with substantially outlying compositions. Both HLW-E-Al-6 and -8 contain 18.20 wt%
CaO while the other glasses in the dataset incorporate only 14.08 ≤ wt% CaO. Calcium was
added in testing of high alumina glass formulations in place of sodium to minimize nepheline
formation. These two glasses were subsequently removed before modeling, leaving 584 glasses
in the dataset.

 To support data-partitioning validation of the EC models, the 584 glasses were divided
into a modeling subset and a validation subset. As discussed in Section 5.1, the validation subset
from Phase 3 was retained in order to facilitate comparison of model prediction performances.
Consequently, all ORP-2018 glasses were used in model fitting and the validation subset was
relatively small in size, comprising only about 10% of all available data. The present modeling
subset consisted of all the modeling glasses from the data-partitioning validation approach during
development of the WTP baseline models and all the subsequent statistically designed
augmentation matrices. It also included actively designed glasses that were randomly selected.
As a result, there are 520 glasses in the modeling subset and 64 glasses in the validation subset.
The glasses in the validation subset for data-partitioning have been identified in an earlier report
[24].

 5.3.2 Updated Electrical Conductivity Model

 Results from earlier modeling efforts supported the finding that a global model showed
good model fitting and validation performances for EC. This is the case even for the greatly
expanded compositional space of the combined WTP/ORP database, and the Phase 3 global EC
model has better fit and validation statistics than the WTP baseline EC model [2, 24]. It was,
therefore, concluded that local models would not offer any significant advantages or
improvements over a global model for EC prediction and that only global models would be
considered.

 The present update and refinement of the global EC model made use of the expanded
dataset and followed the previous model development approach. The model form considered was
a truncated T2 PQM model on the natural logarithm of the EC. The advantages of using the
logarithmic transformation in EC modeling have been discussed previously [2]. The model form
for the EC model is given by:

 q q q −1 q  q xi
                ln( EC ) =  a i x i + Selected   a ii x i2 +   a ij x i x j  +  bi             ,   (5.11)
                                                i =1                             i =1 (T / 1000 )
 2
 i =1 i  j

where ln(EC) denotes the natural logarithm of EC (in S/cm), xi (i = 1, 2, …, q) is the normalized
mass fractions of the q glass oxides such that the sum of all oxides equals to 1, while ai, aii, aij,
and bi are coefficients to be estimated, and T is the temperature (in K).

 The modeling approach was similar to that described above for PCT model development,
where selection of the linear terms was accomplished by comparing the mean response value
(ln(EC) of –1.375 [EC = 0.235 S/cm] for 520 glasses and 2080 temperature-EC data points) with

 45

the 99%-confidence intervals of the model parameter estimates. The non-linear terms were then
evaluated using JMP® RS methodologies on the modeling dataset. The RS method was used to
identify both the significant cross-product and squared terms. Stepwise regression allowed
selection of the non-linear terms based on the p-values calculated from the Student t-tests. The
regression continued until all non-linear terms in the model had p-values lower than 0.0001 to
minimize the number of model terms. Both data-partitioning validation and k-fold cross
validation (k = 5) were performed for the EC models developed.

 Table 5.18 lists the coefficients, their standard deviations, p-values for the non-linear
terms, and performance statistics of the updated global EC model developed with 520 HLW
glasses. The Phase 3 global EC model fitting 453 glasses is included in Table 5.18 for
comparison. The updated model is a 26-term truncated T2 linear mixture (LM) model and
consists of one more linear term (K2O) than the Phase 3 global model.

 The fit regression and k-fold validation statistics of the updated model R2 = 0.941, R2A =
0.940, R2P = 0.939, RMSE = 0.159, R2k-fold = 0.939 are almost identical to those found for the
Phase 3 model. Model validation using data partition and the same validation subset (R2 = 0.906
and RMSEv = 0.158), however, show a small improvement over Phase 3 and approaches the R2
statistic for k-fold validation. Figures 5.20 and 5.21 compare, respectively, the PvM EC values
for 520 modeling glasses and 64 validation glasses. Figure 5.20 shows relatively tight scatter
about the 45º line, with only moderately outlying data for glasses HLW07-28, HLW07-40, and
HLW18-04 (see Section 4.3). In contrast, the scatter for the validation glasses is more noticeable
in Figure 5.21.

 The main model evaluation and validation statistics for selected EC models are
summarized in Table 5.19. These statistics are the results of model development conducted in
several phases over a period of more than 10 years. The HLW database used to develop these
models has grown from 208 HLW glasses (for the WTP baseline model) to the present 584
glasses, while the compositional space covered has been greatly expanded. The statistics in Table
5.19 are based on model developments that (i) employed datasets of different sizes and
compositional ranges, (ii) explored global approach only, and (iii) performed validation by data-
splitting (k-fold validation) and data-partitioning. The statistics in Table 5.19 show that the
global truncated T2 linear mixture model form continues to give good modeling and validation
performance, confirming the previous finding that local EC models may not provide any
improvements or advantages over global models, even for a much enlarged composition space.
Figure 5.22 compares the adjusted R2 statistics for the global EC models; the adjusted R2
statistics are more suited for comparison than the R2 statistics over models with different number
of parameters and modeling glasses by including the degrees of freedom in the calculation.

 5.4.1 Viscosity Dataset

another eight glasses yielded viscosity data at fewer than four temperatures for each glass; these

randomly selected. As a result, there are 471 glasses in the modeling subset and 67 glasses in the
validation subset. The glasses in the validation subset for data-partitioning have been identified
in an earlier report [24].

 5.4.2 Updated Viscosity Model

 Results from earlier modeling efforts supported the finding that a global model showed
good model fitting and validation performance for viscosity. This is the case even for the greatly
expanded compositional space of the combined WTP/ORP database, and the Phase 3 global
viscosity model has similar fit and validation statistics when compared to the WTP baseline
viscosity model [2, 24]. It was, therefore, unlikely that local models would offer any significant
advantages or improvements over a global model for viscosity prediction and only global model
was considered.

 The present update and refinement of the global viscosity model made use of the
expanded dataset and followed the previous model development approach. The model form
considered was a truncated T2 PQM model on the natural logarithm of the viscosity. The
advantages of using the logarithmic transformation in viscosity modeling have been discussed
previously [2]. The model form for the viscosity model is given by:

 q
 q q −1 q
  q xi
 ln( ) =  a i xi + Selected  a ii xi + a ij xi x j  +  bi
 2
 2
 , (5.12)
                         i =1                 i =1      i   j            i =1 (T / 1000 )

 47

where ln(η) denotes the natural logarithm of viscosity (in P), xi (i = 1, 2, …, q) is the normalized
mass fractions of the q glass oxides such that the sum of all oxides equals to 1, ai, aii, aij, and bi
are coefficients to be estimated, and T is the temperature (in K).

 The modeling approach was similar to that described above for PCT model development,
where selection of the linear terms was accomplished by comparing the mean response value
(ln(η) of 4.341 [η = 76.79 P] for 471 glasses and 1884 temperature-viscosity data points) with the
99%-confidence intervals of the model parameter estimates. The non-linear terms were then
evaluated using JMP® RS methodologies on the modeling dataset. The RS method was used to
identify both the significant cross-product and squared terms. Stepwise regression allowed
selection of the non-linear terms based on the p-values calculated from the Student t-tests. The
regression continued until all non-linear terms in the model had p-values lower than 0.0001 to
minimize the number of model terms. Both data-partitioning validation and k-fold cross
validation (k = 5) were performed for the viscosity models developed.

 Table 5.20 gives the coefficients, their standard deviations, p-values for the non-linear
terms, and performance statistics of the updated global viscosity model developed with 471
HLW glasses. The Phase 3 global viscosity model fitting 412 glasses is included in Table 5.20
for comparison. The updated model is a 25-term truncated T2 linear mixture model and has four
fewer terms than the Phase 3 global model.

 The fit regression and k-fold validation statistics of the updated model R2 = 0.975, R2A =
0.974, R2P = 0.974, RMSE = 0.215, R2k-fold = 0.974 are very similar to those found for the Phase
3 model. Model validation using data partition and the same validation subset (R2 = 0.978 and
RMSEv = 0.191) also has essentially the same performance as the Phase 3 model and the same
R2 statistic as k-fold validation. Figures 5.23 and 5.24 compare, respectively, the PvM viscosity
values for 471 modeling glasses and 67 validation glasses. Figure 5.24 shows the good data-
partitioning validation performance of the updated model, with tight scatter about the 45º line,
which represents perfect prediction. It is also seen that it is preferable to include more glasses
with higher viscosity in the validation subset.

 The main model evaluation and validation statistics for selected viscosity models are
summarized in Table 5.21. These statistics are the results of model developments conducted in
several phases over a period of more than 10 years. The HLW database used to develop these
models has grown from 197 HLW glasses (for the WTP baseline model) to the present 538
glasses, while the compositional space covered has been greatly expanded. The statistics in Table
5.19 are based on model developments that (i) employed datasets of different sizes and
compositional ranges, (ii) explored global approach only, and (iii) performed validation by data-
splitting (k-fold validation) and data-partitioning. The statistics in Table 5.21 show that the
global truncated T2 linear mixture model form continues to give good modeling and validation
performance, confirming the previous finding that local viscosity models may not provide any
improvements or advantages over global models, even for a much enlarged composition space.
Figure 5.22 compares the adjusted R2 statistics for the global viscosity models; the adjusted R2
statistics are more suited for comparison than the R2 statistics over models with different number
of parameters and modeling glasses by including the degrees of freedom in the calculation.

 48

5.5 TCLP-Cd release Model

 5.5.1 TCLP-Cd release Dataset

 Of the ORP-2018 glasses collected and discussed in Sections 2 and 4, 38 HLW18 glasses
have data on TCLP-Cd release (see Table 4.1 and Table 4.11); the other ORP-2018 glasses did
not contain CdO. Table 4.2 shows that overall, there are 270 HLW glasses with TCLP-Cd release
data in the combined WTP/ORP database. These data were collected by three different
laboratories: Severn Trent Laboratories (STL)-Knoxville and STL-St. Louis performed TCLP
testing on the WTP glasses to support delisting compliance, while VSL conducted all TCLP
testing on the ORP glasses. The total number of combined WTP/ORP HLW glasses available for
TCLP Cd-modeling is 267, after exclusion of glasses that were considered response outliers in
previous modeling efforts. The excluded glasses are HLW03-01, HLW03-08, and HLW13-05 [1,
22]. A review of the data in Table 4.11 showed that HLW18-16 had unusually high TCLP-Cd
release, as already noted in Section 4.5. Preliminary modeling investigation, which included
fitting the ORP-2018 data to the Phase 3 TCLP-Cd release model, also suggested that HLW18-
16 was an extreme outlier. It was subsequently removed from the modeling dataset, which
consisted of 266 glasses. All 266 glasses were used in fitting the updated TCLP-Cd model and
data-partitioning validation was not performed in order to use all available data in modeling.

 5.5.2 Updated TCLP-Cd Release Model

 Previous models to relate TCLP-Cd release (unnormalized release cCd or normalized
release rCd, as defined in Section 4.5) to glass composition investigated only global models in
various model forms [1, 24]; local models were not developed. The WTP baseline TCLP-Cd
model was developed using weighted least squares (WLS) regression on the WTP dataset
because of the different measurement uncertainties associated with the two laboratories that
collected the data. Replicate TCLP testing at VSL was limited to one glass and no strong basis
existed to determine the uncertainties and any bias. Previous TCLP-Cd release modeling at VSL
therefore did not employ WLS regression and the Phase 3 model was a PQM model for the
normalized release given by:

 q
 q q −1 q
 
 ln( rCd ) =  bi xi + Selected  bii xi2 + bij xi x j  , (5.13)
                                        i =1                i =1      i  j         

where ln(rCd) denotes the natural logarithm of normalized TCLP-Cd release in g/l, the xi (i = 1, 2,
…, q) is the normalized mass fractions of the q glass oxides such that their sum equals to 1,
while bi, bii, are bij are coefficients to be estimated.

 The modeling approach to update the TCLP-Cd release model was identical to that of
Phase 3 and the linear terms were selected by comparing the mean response value (-2.752 for
266 glasses) to the 99%-confidence intervals of the model parameter estimates. The non-linear
terms in the components of the model were then evaluated using JMP® RS methodologies on the

 49

The Catholic University of America Enhancement of HLW Glass Property-Composition Models
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-19R4480-1, Rev. 0

dataset. The RS method was used to identify both the significant cross-product and squared
terms. Stepwise regression allowed selection of the non-linear terms based on the p-values
calculated from the Student t-tests. The regression continued until all non-linear terms in the
model had p-values lower than 0.05 to minimize the number of model terms. Only k-fold cross
validation (k = 5) was performed.

 The updated TCLP-Cd release model developed with 266 glasses to ln(rCd) values is a
PQM model with 14 linear and 12 non-linear terms. Table 5.22 lists the model coefficients, their
standard deviations, p-values for the quadratic terms, and summary statistics for model fitting
and k-fold validation. Table 5.22 also includes the Phase 3 TCL-Cd model for comparison. The
updated model has fit statistics R2 = 0.826, R2A = 0.808, R2P = 0.773 and RMSE = 0.398, all of
which are very comparable to those for the Phase 3 model. The k-fold validation R2 = 0.763
shows an improvement. Figure 5.25 is the PvM plot for the 266 HLW glasses using the updated
TCLP-Cd release model. Note that the Group 2 high-alumina glasses continue to have higher Cd
releases and more data scatter.

 The model evaluation and k-fold validation statistics for the TCLP-Cd releases models
that were developed since Phase 1 are summarized in Table 5.23. All are global models that
cover datasets of different sizes and compositional ranges. Further, the modeling data were
collected at three different laboratories. No data-partitioning validation was performed because
there was a general lack of data. Table 5.23 shows that the fit statistics worsened as the
composition region of interest expanded, with the model LOF becoming increasingly significant
and the RMSE values growing larger. For the updated model, the LOF is significant at the 96%
confidence level. This comparison strongly suggests that development of local models should be
explored in the future, when a larger data set is available. Figure 5.26 compares the adjusted R2
statistics for the TCLP-Cd release models; the adjusted R2 statistics are more suited for
comparison than the R2 statistics over models with different number of parameters and modeling
glasses by including the degrees of freedom in the calculation.

 50

 SECTION 6.0
 SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS

 Since the development of the WTP baseline property-composition models [2], VSL has
developed a wide range of HLW glass formulations for ORP that would allow significantly
higher waste loadings and/or higher glass production rates. In order to realize the full benefits of
these ORP glass formulations, the WTP baseline models must be revised and enhanced to
incorporate the new data. During earlier model enhancement work, new HLW glasses were
statistically designed, fabricated and characterized to update the database and the property-
composition models [22-24]. Although the new glass data provided better coverage and
underpinning of the models, the gain in model performance was limited, as judged by the key
statistics of regression and model validation [24]. This is not surprising in view of the very large
high-dimensional composition space and the limited number of new glasses. The primary
objectives of the present enhancement work included further augmentation of the HLW property-
composition database and subsequent updating of previously developed models in order to
improve their performance. A major difference between the present enhancement work and
earlier work was in the design of the augmentation test matrices. Instead of identifying and
filling compositional gaps, the new test matrix (HLW18 glasses) was statistically designed to
strategically place glass formulations in composition regions where relatively large errors were
projected by the latest HLW property-composition models [24]. In this way, the goal of the new
design was to reduce prediction errors of the models.

 Characterization of the 40 HLW18 glasses was performed to gather data on PCT (B, Li,
and Na) releases, spinel T1%, electrical conductivity, viscosity, TCLP-Cd releases, and nepheline
formation upon CCC. These data were collected, together with those for 97 actively designed
glasses from other tasks [27-30], into the combined WTP/ORP database and used to update the
HLW property-composition models. The update of the nepheline formation models with the new
CCC data was the subject of a separate task [41] and is not covered in this work. As summarized
in Table 5.1, the total number of HLW glasses available to support model development has now
grown to nearly 270 (for TCLP-Cd release) and 680 (for PCT-B and -Na). While these represent
very sizable increases over the respective WTP datasets used to develop the baseline models,
they should also be viewed from the perspective of the vastly expanded composition spaces
being covered. Many composition sub-regions still remain sparsely populated, as can be seen in
Figure 2.4.

 The PCT release data for the ORP-2018 glasses are discussed in Section 4. These glasses
have PCT-B, -Li, and -Na releases that are generally higher than those of existing HLW glasses
(see Figure 4.2). Earlier efforts to model PCT releases suffered from a relative lack of HLW
glasses with large PCT releases; the resulting models did not give accurate predictions in the
high-release regions and required bias correction [2, 24]. While the new dataset is comparatively
small, it is a valuable addition to the combined database. The combined database was used to
update the Phase 3 global and local PCT-B, PCT-Li, and PCT-Na models. The partial cubic
mixture models were not considered since they have been shown to give no improvements over

 51

the partial quadratic mixture models [2, 24]. The updated Global PCT-B, -Li, and -Na models all
show very similar fit statistics to those of the corresponding Phase 3 Global models, which is not
surprising since the data additions are small in size compared to existing data. In contrast, the
prediction performances of all updated global models, based on data-partition validation of the
same subset of separate glasses, show moderate improvements over the respective Phase 3 global
models, giving better validation R2 and validation RMSE statistics. They are also comparable to
the data-partition validation performances of the WTP baseline PCT models (better validation R 2
for PCT-B and PCT-Li but worse validation R2 for PCT-Na), in spite of covering considerably
larger composition ranges (the WTP validation subset consisted of fewer glasses and covered
smaller composition regions). The revised Local 1 (Al2O3 ≤ 13.1 wt%) models for PCT-B, -Li,
and -Na are all comparable to corresponding Phase 3 Local 1 models, with similar goodness-of-
fit statistics. The updated Local 2 (Al2O3 > 13.1 wt%) models, however, perform slightly worse
than the Phase 3 models. Nevertheless, the Local 2 models have the best modeling performance
when compared to the global and Local 1 models, based on a comparison of modeling adjusted
R2 statistics. This strongly suggests that development of local models should be continued in the
future over smaller HLW glass composition spaces where they are likely to fit and predict better.

 The global model for predicting spinel T1% was updated with a modeling subset of 410
glasses and a validation subset of 83 glasses. Compared to the Phase 3 global model, the updated
model has somewhat worse goodness-of-fit statistics but the performance statistics (R2v and
RMSEv) are better when validating the same validation subset of glasses. In addition, the updated
global model continues to have large RMSE, which is attributed to the significant uncertainty in
measuring vol% crystallinity of heat-treated samples and the relatively large number of
temperature/vol% data points needed in T1% estimation [2]. Improvements of the spinel T1%
model performance in terms of reducing the associated uncertainty therefore will demand
sufficient number of data points to support more precise estimates of T1%. As expected from the
large overlaps in the modeling datasets (only 13 Group 2 glasses were added in the update), both
updated Local 1 and Local 2 models have evaluation statistics similar to those for the
corresponding Phase 3 local models. Both updated local models give better fits to the data than
the updated global model (Figure 5.19), an observation that has been made previously [24]. As is
the case with PCT modeling, development of local models to cover smaller compositional
regions will be more likely to yield spinel T1% models that give better fitting and prediction
performances.

 Updates of the EC and viscosity models were supported with the addition of 67 and 59
HLW glasses (268 and 236 data points), respectively, to the database. Only global partial
quadratic mixture models were considered since these models performed well in fitting and
validation, while both local and cubic models did not provide any significant improvements or
advantages, as documented before [24]. The present results offer further verification of the
earlier findings and a comparison of modeling adjusted R2 in Figure 5.22 clearly shows little
differences in regression statistics among the WTP baseline and subsequently enhanced models.
The prediction performances of the updated EC and viscosity models, based on data-partition
validation of the same subset of separate glasses, show slight improvements over the respective
Phase 3 global models, giving better validation R2 and RMSE statistics. The validation R2
statistics for both EC and viscosity models approach the respective modeling R2 values, showing

 52

that the models can be used to make accurate predictions for glasses not used in their
development.

 The new data were used to update the global TCLP-Cd release model. Due to the smaller
data set that is available for this property, data-partitioning validation was not performed in
updating the global TCLP-Cd release model. Evaluation of the global PQM models based on
comparing fit and k-fold validation statistics shows that the updated model has significant LOF,
despite having similar modeling R2 and better k-fold validation R2 when compared to the Phase 3
model. Furthermore, it is shown that the fit and validation statistics worsened as the composition
region of interest expanded, with the model LOF becoming increasingly significant and the
RMSE values growing larger. Therefore, in order to improve the performance in predicting
TCLP-Cd releases in HLW glasses, development of local models needs to be explored.

 The present model enhancement work focused on reducing the prediction errors of HLW
property-composition models, particularly the models for PCT releases and spinel T1% because
they have been found previously to have the largest errors in model fitting and model validation
[24]. The updated global models for these properties all show modest improvements in
predictions over the Phase 3 global models, as determined by the summary statistics in validation
with the same subset of separate glasses. The goodness-of-fit statistics for the updated models,
however, do not show any large improvements and actually become worse in the cases of PCT-B
release and spinel T1%. The updated local models for PCT releases and spinel T1%, especially for
the high-alumina region, continue to give performance comparable to or better than the global
models, suggesting that developing local models should be the preferred approach in the future.
The updated EC and viscosity models further improve upon the respective Phase 3 models,
which can be used to give good predictions, to give better validation R2 and validation RMSE.
New data with the required quality assurance that become available in the future should support
the refining and validating of the global models of these properties since it is unlikely that local
models will provide any substantial improvements or advantages. Finally, improvements of the
TCLP-Cd release models should consider alternate development approaches, including local
models and perhaps alternative model forms.

 53

 SECTION 7.0
 QUALITY ASSURANCE

 This work was conducted under a quality assurance program compliant with the
applicable criteria of 10 CFR 830.120; Office of Civilian Waste Management Department of
Energy (DOE)/RW-0333P, Quality Assurance Requirements and Description (QARD), Revision
20; the American Society of Mechanical Engineers (ASME) Nuclear Quality Assurance (NQA)-
1-2008 including NQA-1a 2009 Addenda; and DOE Order 414.1 D, Quality Assurance. The VSL
QA program is supplemented by a Quality Assurance Project Plan for ORP work that is
conducted at VSL [49]. Test and procedure requirements by which the testing activities are
planned and controlled are also defined in this plan. The program is supported by VSL standard
operating procedures that were used for this work [50]. Requirements of DOE/RW-0333P are
applicable to the glass composition and PCT portions of the HLW work.
